How 9/11 Helped Shape Our Politics Today
2026-09-11

How 9/11 Helped Shape Our Politics Today

TIME | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The World Trade Center twin towers with Staten Island in the background in New York, New York, August 5, 1972. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings From the moment they occurred, the terror attacks of  Sept. 11, 2001 were seen as inaugurating a new age of asymmetric violence and warfare. President George W. Bush described the war on terror that followed as a generational conflict. Yet, for all the transformations it produced, Al-Qaeda and Islamic militancy more broadly have, in my view, receded within a decade. Not so much because it was defeated on the battlefield but because it was unable to gain much support among Muslims. When popular uprisings swept the Arab world in the early 2010s, protesters in Tunisia, Egypt, Syria, Bahrain, and elsewhere in the region took to the streets not in the name of Islam, but democracy. The Arab Spring, more than any military victory, signaled the decline of militancy. Al Qaeda faced its greatest defeat at Tahrir Square. Islamic militancy flourished principally on the battlefields of war on terror, in countries such as Afghanistan and Iraq, a trajectory epitomized by the emergence of the Islamic State from within the American prison system in Iraq. While Islamic militancy still survives in pockets of weak or fractured states, elsewhere it has petered out into the province of petty criminals or psychologically disturbed individuals. What, then, does its brief and brutal career mean for world history? The dramatic arrival of Al-Qaeda and Islamic militancy in the global public sphere in the early 2000s did not mark the beginning of a new chapter of global history. Perhaps that confrontation between Al-Qaeda and America signaled a historical ending: the true conclusion of the Cold War. By the fall of 2001, the Soviet Union and the bipolar world had been dead for a decade. The Cold War survived in the institutions and practices it had created: NATO, the United Nations and its conventions, international courts and the multilateral institutions established to knit together a divided world through systems of deterrence, development and compromise born of the fear of an atomic holocaust. For a while, these institutions appeared capable of operating without their founding cause, but that hope proved illusory. In the 1990s, wars swept across the Balkans, the Caucasus, the Middle East and Africa. Al-Qaeda arose at the confluence of post-Cold War globalization and unipolar violence. With its emergence at the turn of the millennium, Al-Qaeda, I argue, brought the Cold War to a definitive close by overturning its central presuppositions. The war on terror rendered the familiar categories of the Cold War—the balance of power, deterrence, great-power rivalry, and hegemony—increasingly obsolete. Fought in civilian spaces against nonstate actors dispersed around the globe, the war on terror also ended up destroying most of the Cold War’s remaining ideological states in the Middle East, including the socialist republics of Iraq, Libya, and Syria. Al-Qaeda's emergence also marked the end of Islamism, which was itself a Cold War movement dedicated to revolution and the creation of ideological states. Islamism’s only durable strongholds, Iran and Afghanistan, came to embody its takeover by clerical establishments of the kind it had once sought to displace. The war on terror also weakened the liberal state, another creature of the Cold War, whose animating purpose had been to pose capitalist liberty against Communist dictatorship. Osama bin Laden used to claim that Westerners thought their interventions in the Middle East would remake the region’s states in the images of their own, but instead it was the Americans and Europeans who came to resemble their despotic and corrupt clients in the region. One of the goals of Al-Qaeda’s campaign of terror was to precisely achieve that by showing how even violence falling short of a conventional war and posing no genuine existential threat was sufficient in provoking Western democracies to dismantle the civil liberties of their own citizens, exposing their claims to freedom as hypocritical. As the war on terror dragged on, Al-Qaeda’s leaders portrayed the anti-terror laws and the increasingly militarized conduct of the U.S. and its allies not as signs of resolve and strength but as displays of weakness and proof of the shallowness of Western liberalism. Bin Laden might have been disappointed by Al-Qaeda’s inability to mobilize enough Muslims around the world to overthrow their dictatorships, but the U.S. and its European allies, which sustained those authoritarian regimes, sadly behaved much as he had predicted. A sacrificial, if not nihilistic, movement whose iconic figure was the suicide bomber, Al-Qaeda had little interest in conquest and government. One of its major objectives was to provoke Western states into attacking their own democracies, in a kind of auto-immune response to terrorism, in which the effort to defend the political body corroded it from within. In that particular sense, bin Laden was perversely successful in dragging America and its allies into expensive wars and weakening their liberal norms as they chose widespread use of emergency powers, surveillance, indefinite detention, torture, and rendition. The war also brought suspicion of Muslims, hostility toward immigration, civilizational rhetoric, and border militarization from the political margins to the center of Western public discourse. America’s preoccupation with the war on terror did squander its unipolar decade. It wasn’t a coincidence that the rise of China—and the China shock—came a few years after the Great Recession of 2008 and a decade after the 9/11 attacks. The cultural and political landscape of Europe and America today—characterized by polarization, populism, and a pervasive distrust of institutions—can be understood, at least in part, as the legacy of the response to the 9/11 attacks by Al-Qaeda. By this measure, my assertion would be that bin Laden won the war on terror much as he had predicted he would. The rise of populism and far-right politics in America and Europe has generated great laments for the corrosion of a golden age of liberalism and progress following the victory of the Allied powers against fascism in World War II. The flowering of postwar liberal democracy owed less to the defeat of fascism than to the Cold War, when the existence of a socialist alternative compelled Europe and North America to emphasize social protections, greater equality, and meaningful political rights. Thus, the crisis of liberal democracy after 9/11 might be better understood not simply as a blowback from the war on terror, but as an aftershock of socialism’s collapse. After the collapse of the Soviet bloc, that pressure disappeared. Though liberal institutions survived formally, the social and economic settlement that sustained them had begun to erode. During World War II and in its immediate aftermath, the Allies were hardly exemplars of liberal standards. Several allied powers were colonial empires engaged in the violent suppression of nationalist movements in Asia and Africa even as they fought fascism. Nor can any account of Allied conduct ignore the firebombing of German cities or the atomic destruction of Hiroshima and Nagasaki. Although the war in Europe and the Pacific dominates debates about Allied misconduct, the colonial character of World War II may tell us more about the origins of our present moment. Colonial armies fought in every theater and constituted some of the forces deployed during the war. At least a million soldiers fighting in the British and French armies against fascism were Muslims. Civilians in territories like India, though far from the principal battlefields, also suffered immensely during the war. The Bengal famine, in which some three million people, Hindus and Muslims, starved to death, was, as many have argued, caused in part by wartime disruptions to grain supplies and by imperial policy. Europe didn’t only take men and material from the colonies for the Great War, but also a repertoire of its illiberal practices in the colonies. The Nazis drew upon precedents established in colonial wars waged by European powers. The Allies, in turn, brought home methods of coercion that had previously been largely confined to their empires. Among them was the revival of medieval doctrines of just war, which subordinated contractual obligations to unilateral claims of moral authority—doctrines that resurfaced during the war on terror to justify pre-emptive attacks and torture. World War II should be understood not simply as liberalism’s triumph over tyranny but also as a source—and continuation—of Western illiberalism, much like what came to the surface after 9/11. It was the Cold War that provided the real foundation of mid-century liberalism. One might even argue that the post-9/11order, from which today’s far-right movements partly emerged, has its roots in World War II’s struggle for global hegemony, a contest suspended for the duration of the Cold War. The war on terror represented a failed effort to impose a unipolar order on the world, one that could no longer rely on the economic instrumentality of globalization alone. And its failure also discredited the globalization associated with free trade and mass migration. In its place arose far-right movements in Western Europe and North America dedicated to dividing the world into racial and cultural blocs, divisions often defended in the language of the struggle against terrorism and Islam. In America, this political vision harks back to the 19th-century Monroe Doctrine; in Europe, it reaches further, to the myth of a culturally homogeneous Christian continent. In both cases, the animating force is not social conservatism alone but hostility toward immigrants, and toward Muslims in particular. Its deeper history may therefore lie in decolonization, which transformed immigration into a defining political reality distinct from the colonial systems of slavery, indenture and managed labor migration that preceded it. Asian and African decolonization not only overlapped with the Cold War but was, in many important ways, made possible by it. As the rival superpowers courted newly independent countries, they helped secure what sovereignty many of those states possessed. Yet decolonization, no less than the Cold War, also made possible the European Economic Community and, eventually, the European Union. Europe emerged from World War II with most of its colonial possessions more or less intact. It was only decolonization that allowed the continent to cohere into a new kind of economic and political unit. Immigration has a double history in America, proceeding from two waves of decolonization: the 19th century independence of Latin American countries and that of Asian and African nations in the 20th century. American anti-immigrant politics consequently differs from the European version, remaining more closely bound to the Monroe Doctrine and its vision of hemispheric control. Europe may possess the older political history, but its encounter with immigration is more recent, starting with labor migration to help with European reconstruction after World War II and expanding again in the wake of the Cold War. The end of the Cold War weakened the sovereignty of many postcolonial states, producing new waves of migration as those countries faced economic pressure or were drawn into conflict. It also reduced European countries from strategic partners in the Cold War to increasingly dependent allies—a relationship laid bare by the war on terror, which expanded American power while disproportionately rewarding American companies. The end of the Cold War, made visible through 9/11 and its aftermath, has also transformed Europe’s understanding of its past. While World War II is still invoked, especially by liberals, it no longer occupies its former place as the 20th century’s defining event and the founding moment of the international order now coming apart. The ascendance of immigration—and, with it, race and culture—as the central political concern on both sides of the Atlantic suggests that decolonization has displaced the war in the historical imagination. Narratives about the struggles against fascism or Communism still shape Western identities, but political debate is increasingly dominated by fears of what might be called “reverse colonization.” Europe’s unwillingness or inability to reckon with colonialism may explain its strange, even pathological identification with the anxieties of its former subjects. The fear of foreign settlement, demographic change, and cultural swamping all echo the language once used in colonized societies of Asia and Africa, where the Christian West was regarded as precisely such a threat. This switching of places is apparent in portrayals of Islam as both an alien and more virile force than Western liberalism, a force whose supposed cohesion and will to dominate must be countered by weapons of solidarity and domination. European politics has thus increasingly begun to appropriate the defensive posture of the peoples Europe once ruled. Perverse though this identification may be, it reveals the extent to which decolonization—itself part of the Cold War’s history— has become the century’s central historical drama. This switching of places is especially true of Islam seen as an alien but also more virile force than Western liberalism, one whose power thus needs to be countered by its own weapons of solidarity and domination. For all the spectacle of its asymmetric attacks, Al Qaeda—and Islamist militancy more broadly—never had more than some tens of thousands of committed supporters worldwide. These movements did not emerge from some hermetically sealed Islamic history. They belonged to global events and narratives, which gave them what influence they possessed. The disproportion between their small numbers and enormous consequences cannot be explained primarily by technology, mass media or even the war on terror. It arose from the institutional and political vulnerabilities exposed by the Cold War’s belated conclusion. We now live amid the detritus of the end of the Cold War and are able to recognize decolonization as the crucial event of the 20th century. Its legacy animates both movements seeking greater inclusion and campaigns promising to restore a national unity defined in racial or cultural terms. More than three decades after the Soviet Union’s collapse, we still live in the wake of the Cold War, an aftermath that first revealed its force in the attacks of Sept. 11, 2001 on New York and Washington, D.C.

Yemen’s Houthis Reach Strategic Island in Key Shipping Route
2026-09-11

Yemen’s Houthis Reach Strategic Island in Key Shipping Route

TIME | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings People sit in a fishing boat sailing the Bab el-Mandeb Strait off of Yemen's southern Taiz province on July 25, 2026. Yemen’s Iran-backed Houthi rebels have reportedly captured the strategic Perim Island that sits in the Bab el-Mandeb Strait as they aim to tighten their grip on the critical maritime chokepoint. The Houthis took Perim on Friday, four Yemeni government sources told Reuters. The capture was also relayed to the Associated Press by a senior military official with Yemen’s internationally recognized government. The reports come a day after the Houthis captured Yemen’s port city of Mokha from Saudi-backed government forces. Perim Island divides the Bab el-Mandeb Strait, a vital trade route situated between Yemen and Djibouti and Eritrea in the Horn of Africa, that links Asia and Europe via the Red Sea and Suez Canal. Its capture would risk a grave escalation in the widening regional conflict. If the Houthis were to assume control of the Bab el-Mandeb, it could give Iran an advantage in its war with the U.S. The active hostilities between Washington and Tehran are largely focused on another critical trade route, the Strait of Hormuz. Read More: Will Iran Target Another Maritime Chokepoint? The Bab El-Mandeb Strait Is Under Threat In a statement shared Friday, Houthi military spokesman Yahya Sarea declared the “success” of a military operation launched on Sept. 3 that intended to expel "Saudi-backed forces" along the western coast of Yemen. He went on to assert that “maritime navigation is safe for all companies, with the exception of Saudi ships.” The statement made no mention of Perim Island. The escalation comes after weeks of fighting between the Houthis and the Saudi-backed government of Yemen, shattering a four-year informal cease-fire in the country's civil war. Yemen’s government on Thursday reportedly launched airstrikes west of Taiz in response to the capture of Mokha and warned civilians to avoid roads between Taiz and Mokha. Houthi-owned Al-Masirah TV reported that Saudi-backed forces launched 40 airstrikes on the provinces of Taiz, Jawf, Hodeida, and Marib. Observers and Yemeni residents fear the escalating fighting could renew an all-out civil war in Yemen. “This renewed war should alarm us all,” Hans Grundberg, the U.N. special envoy for Yemen, said at an emergency meeting of the U.N. Security Council on Thursday. “It effectively marks the end of the relative calm that Yemen has experienced for over four years since the U.N.-brokered truce.” Capturing the southwestern port and Perim—also known as Mayun—would significantly strengthen the Houthis’ ability to threaten shipping via the Bab el-Mandeb, a waterway through which 8.1 million oil barrels a day passed during the second quarter of 2026, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration. The Houthis previously disrupted shipping in 2024, in response to Israel’s occupation and bombardment of Gaza, and made a global impact. The move prompted the U.S. and U.K. to carry out strikes against Houthi rebel targets. The Bab el-Mandeb has become even more important since Iran militarized the Strait of Hormuz in retaliation for the U.S. and Israel’s Feb. 28 strikes. Andreas Krieg, a senior lecturer of defense studies at King’s College London, tells TIME that any Houthi takeover of these Red Sea areas would be “hugely significant" for the wider region. In the 2024 disruption, the Houthis “were striking shipping from further inland, now they're controlling the coastline, very close to shipping, particularly Perim Island,” he says. Perim offers a “vantage point from which the Houthis can operate with far less sophisticated technology, drones, and missiles in order to strike shipping,” Krieg says, adding that “they could even use small skiffs in a sort piracy fashion to harass shipping.” The Houthis reportedly received direct guidance and weapons from Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC). Iran reportedly told the Houthis last week to escalate their attacks on Saudi Arabia. Iran publicly maintains that the Houthis are “not a proxy.” The country’s Foreign Ministry said in a statement Friday that the conflict in Yemen must be resolved through dialogue instead of military action. Iran’s First Vice President Mohammad Reza Aref on Friday said that Tehran was “delighted by the victories of the heroic Yemeni nation” and put a call to unity “around the banner of Islam.” Krieg explains how the Houthis may want to keep their targets solely on Saudi shipping in order to not draw out an international coordinated maritime response. “They could widen the conflict to not just Saudi shipping, but any shipping going through the Bab el-Mandeb. But it would be an own-goal for the Houthis, because you're then giving the international community a mandate to basically support the Saudis,” he says. Axios reported Friday that Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman had called Trump twice the previous day to request that he launch strikes against the Houthis—a request Trump is said to have declined. "The United States is focused on protecting our core national security interests—such as ensuring freedom of navigation in the Red Sea—while empowering our regional partners to take the lead in managing and resolving regional security challenges,” a senior Administration official told TIME when asked about the reports. “We are in continuous dialogue with Saudi Arabia and the Republic of Yemen Government regarding regional stability." The Houthis’ advance comes as Tehran has sought new ways to counter Washington’s economic pressure campaign and military attacks. A stronger Houthi position along the Red Sea coast could further constrain global energy supplies, raise shipping and insurance costs, and push up oil prices. Even if the Houthis are acting in their own interest, Iran could gain significant leverage over Washington in conjunction with continued pressure on traffic through the Strait of Hormuz. The capture of Mokha came hours after President Donald Trump on Wednesday suggested that Iran’s government was prolonging the war in an attempt to affect Republican prospects in the U.S. midterm elections in November. He nevertheless insisted Iran is on the verge of collapse and that the war will end “immediately after” the vote. The Houthis are “not necessarily doing the bidding of the Iranians; they're doing what is in their interests. Nobody really controls the Houthis,” says Krieg, but this escalation very well could benefit Tehran in negotiations. “Ultimately this gives, even by extension or by proxy, the Iranians another part to play if they feel their terms are not being met, or if they feel that they need to dial up the pressure,” Krieg concludes. From Mokha, which lies around 80 km north of Bab el-Mandeb, and Perim Island, which sits directly in the waterway around 26 km from shore, the Houthis would have a stronger position to disrupt shipping, particularly targeting vessels travelling to and from Saudi Arabia, which is the world’s biggest crude exporter and a close U.S. ally. Saudi Arabia has routed more oil to its Red Sea port of Yanbu after the effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz. But the Houthis declared a maritime blockade of Saudi Arabia on July 20 and began attacking Saudi shipping and energy infrastructure. Saudi crude exports have reportedly fallen to their lowest level in 13 years, with production dropping 23% from July to August. Overall vessel traffic through Bab el-Mandeb fell around 24% in the week following the July blockade. The kingdom has responded with airstrikes in Yemen’s north and warned Iran to rein in the group. It has supplied its Yemeni allies with arms, intelligence, and logistical assistance. Experts tell TIME that whilst the Houthis have shown their “intent and capability" to strike shipping, the tightening of their grip on the Bab el-Mandeb is sufficient to rattle energy markets and supply chains. “This just adds another layer of uncertainty, very likely increasing the insurance payments, and that will increase shipping costs,” says Krieg, which would make it “very problematic for global energy trade.” The advance could also force the U.S. to devote military resources towards protecting a second maritime chokepoint and assisting its ally, potentially complicating efforts to limit or swiftly end U.S. involvement in the Middle East.
The Atlantic
How Real Is the Threat of AI Starting the Next Pandemic?
2026-09-11

How Real Is the Threat of AI Starting the Next Pandemic?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Last month, a team of scientists published a high-profile paper describing how they’d taught an AI model to help produce brand-new, deadly viruses. The AI—a “genome-language model” trained on DNA sequences—had designed multiple new genomes that scientists then synthesized into functional viruses. And some of those viruses were capable of killing bacteria in a dish. News of AI-made viruses went over poorly. The loudest reaction to the study (which had been released as a preprint about a year ago) was fear. Now that AI can design viruses that go after bacteria, perhaps someone could use the technology to manufacture pathogens that harm people—sparking a major outbreak, even a devastating pandemic. This potential threat evoked some of the same concerns that politicians, researchers, and tech leaders (including Bill Gates) have raised. And these fears are only gaining traction: On Thursday, Anthropic released a report that sparked a round of doom-laden headlines, detailing several instances in which the company banned accounts that were using its LLM, Claude, for what could have been reputable research efforts—or efforts to develop biological weapons. AI, which has repeatedly proved itself capable of exceeding user expectations and bypassing guardrails, is a formidable force; many fears about its potential biological threat stem from its relentless pace of development and the possibility that it could make independent breakthroughs, exceeding researchers’ own understanding, especially if AIs eventually achieve recursive self-improvement—that is, models designing newer and smarter models. Kevin Esvelt, a biologist at MIT and a co-founder of Secure Bio, a biosecurity nonprofit, told me that although he thinks the probability of an AI-driven pandemic remains quite low, the consequences of such a catastrophe would be so large that “it’s very important that we drive that probability down as low as we can.” But many other biologists, especially infectious-disease experts and virologists, told me that the threat of a pandemic need not loom particularly large on the list of AI-made threats. To the scientists most familiar with the minutiae of growing, modifying, and testing viruses in a lab, and the havoc they can wreak in the real world, the viruses that have already arisen through evolution, or will soon enough, still present much more immediate perils. In other words, humans should be investing aggressively in pandemic preparation, but not because of AI. To be clear, viruses made with the help of AI could pose some threat. The experts I spoke with agreed that, in the wrong hands, AI could be used to speed infectious destruction, to a degree. To create the bacterium-killing virus, scientists led by a group at Stanford University and the Arc Institute fed the AI model, called Evo, only viral genomes that attack bacteria. Those genomes are much simpler and smaller than ones coding for viruses that go after humans. In theory, though, with a similar technique, researchers could train AI on the genomes of viruses that infect humans, with the aim of  manufacturing novel pathogens. Or perhaps—as some people worry is likelier, easier, and more dangerous—users could ask AI to adjust specific traits of viruses known to cause disease, until those pathogens can skirt available vaccines. Scientists have long been able to manipulate viral genetics without AI. This kind of “gain of function” research has stirred its own political and scientific controversy—especially in discussions about the origins of COVID-19—and the Trump administration has restricted federal funding for it. But current AI models have the capacity to “put a supreme battery in the back of the things that people were already doing,” Brandon Ogbunu, an evolutionary biologist and infectious-diseases modeler at Yale University, told me. Tom Inglesby, the director of the Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security, worries about bad actors using AI to surmount some of the limits of their own technical expertise. Ideally, models powerful enough to suggest new viral genomes should be subject to strict, formal review, Inglesby said; perhaps they could even be limited to training on only certain types of benign data. (For instance, the team that built Evo trained it only on viruses that kill bacteria, and withheld any data about viruses that can harm more complex life forms, including humans.) Esvelt advocates for restricting access to certain models entirely. Other researchers, however, including the virologists I spoke with, pointed out that another set of safeguards is already in place: how little we humans—the creators and users of AI—understand about pathogenic viruses. For AI to map out more effective pathogens, people must be able to feed it data on what makes viruses highly transmissible, dangerous, and able to circumvent immune safeguards. But these concepts are complex and murky enough that the chasm AI would need to cross is wide; researchers lack much of the basic knowledge they’d need to train an AI properly on such topics. “Right now, the data is junk going in,” Seema Lakdawala, a virologist at Emory University, told me. Generations of virologists have not come up with firm answers to key questions about what makes certain viruses better at causing diseases than others, or why some people are more susceptible to particular infections than others. Even expert virologists may struggle to ask AI the best prompts to guide it in useful directions. In her own research on viral transmission, Lakdawala hasn’t found LLMs such as Claude and ChatGPT particularly helpful or correct, she told me, even when she’s asked it questions far simpler than “How do I make a pandemic virus?” (for example, basic questions about the flu-virus genome). Brian Hie, the lead researcher on the team that designed Evo, told me he’s also unconvinced “that AI would even be the method of choice for someone trying to make a dangerous pathogen.” It does not know enough about pathogens, because we don’t know enough about pathogens. Esvelt, at MIT, argues that AI might not need to understand viruses perfectly, or even that well, to stumble across a dangerous iteration of one. AIs might also improve rapidly enough to make more sense of the existing data than human researchers ever have. But no matter how good AIs get, they so far have no way to build and test new pathogens, Gigi Gronvall, a health-security expert at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, told me. Even a very motivated human—whether they wanted to spark a pandemic for a government or terrorist organization, or for their own ends—would still have to pick through AI-generated possibilities, successfully synthesize them, assess them for enhanced (or at least sufficient) transmissibility and virulence, produce a sufficient quantity to do serious damage, and then release that payload of new virus into the world.  All of that requires substantial skill in the lab, as well as expensive equipment and lab animals. It’s not impossible, but it certainly isn’t easy, either. The practice of building new viruses in a laboratory is fickle: “Even if I give two experienced scientists the same protocol, they’re going to do it differently, and the results could come out differently,” Linsey Marr, a viral-transmission researcher at Virginia Tech, told me. Tinkering with viruses the old-fashioned way has also taught researchers that many hypotheses about what should make a virus more contagious or dangerous don’t pan out. Researchers cannot simply tune up a virus’s transmissibility or virulence like a dial; such changes can come with serious costs, including rendering the entire pathogen nonfunctional—incapable of infecting or hurting a person at all. Evolution is already a master at tinkering with life forms and building upon their most successful—or dangerous—traits. Viruses are constantly trying out new versions of themselves and aren’t limited by human expectations about what “dangerous” agents of disease look like. Evolving pathogens also need no assistance from malevolent actors to start epidemics; in nature, where species constantly mingle, new outbreaks begin all the time. And nature has an elegant way of dealing with mistakes: Unviable iterations of life simply never come to exist at all. As good as AI is getting, “I can’t look anyone in the eye and say that process is any more harmful than what nature is already operating,” Ogbunu, the Yale evolutionary biologist, said. The virologists I spoke with did agree that AI models should be monitored and regulated, but were wary of limitations that would hinder AI’s ability to speed the development of new vaccines, shore up viral surveillance, boost epidemiological modeling, or otherwise benefit public-health and infectious-disease research. (Even the types of bacterium-killing viruses that caused such an uproar could be deployed against antibiotic-resistant infections.) Worries about killer viruses are already interfering with such lines of research. Among the examples of potential threats in Anthropic’s report were gain-of-function work on chikungunya virus and research about how flu viruses adapt to mammals—projects that could have been malicious or, in theory, could improve therapeutics or outbreak detection, respectively. (The company said in the report that it couldn’t confirm whether the queries represented the pursuit of knowledge for the public good or attempts at bioterrorism, and had erred on the side of caution.) Lakdawala and Marr told me that, recently, when they have asked Claude or ChatGPT about, say, genetic quirks in the flu-virus genome or using UV light to inactivate viruses, the AI flagged or halted their conversations. “It happens to me almost daily,” Marr said. (An OpenAI spokesperson wrote over email that “today’s models are specifically trained to withhold biological information that could enable harm” and that researchers “pursuing legitimate biological and life sciences work” can apply to its Trusted Access for Cyber program, which requires proof of identification. Anthropic did not respond to a request for comment.) Lakdawala and Marr are now trying to develop their own framework to help AI models distinguish legitimate scientific pursuits from nefarious ones. Both say that it’s difficult: What would the criteria be? Who would be the final arbiters of those criteria and of ambiguous cases? And none of those guardrails would address the most extreme concerns about viruses created by AI—that a self-improving intelligence might eventually be able to deliberately design, manufacture, and release a pathogen. For now, though, AI is still a tool. We’re still in charge of the questions we ask it, the data we feed it, and the fate of any virus that it might suggest we create.

AI Writing Has Entered Its Post-Shame Era
2026-09-11

AI Writing Has Entered Its Post-Shame Era

The Atlantic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Subscribe here:  Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube AI-infused writing is suddenly everywhere: not just in automated email responses or meeting summaries, but in books and even the pages of mainstream newspapers. When an editor at The Wall Street Journal recently defended an op-ed that was 100 percent written by AI, it felt like a possible turning point, at which people are no longer apologizing. So is AI-assisted writing in its post-shame era? What does that mean for the way we process, communicate, and think? Host Charlie Warzel talks to the journalists Elizabeth Spiers and Teddy Brown about being a writer when the robots are coming for you, and how technology does not have to be inevitable. The following is a transcript of the episode: Elizabeth Spiers: I just worry that AI is making everybody dumber. I think when you outsource the fundamental cognitive arsenal that you’re using to actually construct a good argument or to make a piece of art, what happens to us as a society if everybody’s outsourcing that to AI models? Teddy Brown: When I worked in tech, I was writing 3,000-page papers about really dense B2B-enterprise marketing strategies, or whatever. Yeah, exactly. And I would get to the end, and it would go onto the website: I have no idea what I wrote about. I could not tell you one thing about that, because it was just all flowing through Claude. Charlie Warzel: I’m Charlie Warzel, and this is Galaxy Brain, a show where today we’re gonna talk about artificial intelligence and the thing that I do for a living, which is writing. We’re almost four years into the ChatGPT era, and AI writing is everywhere. It’s flooded the internet. We’ve got SEO spam, slop—all that synthetic pablum that’s just immediately recognizable. A Pew Research analysis from July estimated that “signs of AI authorship can be found in over one-third of web pages published after ChatGPT was released.” One-third of web pages. It’s come for schools; it’s come for universities, which have been struggling for years to get students to write papers without chatbot assistance. And in professional writing, we’ve watched this slow and steady creep of AI show up in book publishing, in mainstream publications, in op-eds. And for a while, the reaction was all the same. As my colleague Will Oremus wrote recently: “Up to now, those accused of passing off a computer’s writing as their own have tended to either apologize or deny it.” In April, James Taranto, who is The Wall Street Journal’s editorial features editor, wrote that “those of us who practice writing as a professional craft have an ethical duty and sometimes a contractual obligation to ensure that our work is original.” Now, a few months later, in the same paper, billionaire Stanley Druckenmiller published an op-ed which was identified as 100 percent written by AI. Only this time, there was no deflecting; there was no apologizing. Druckenmiller and the head of The Wall Street Journal’s opinion pages admitted to and defended the usage of this chatbot to write the op-ed. The head of the opinion page responded: “AI is a fact of modern life.” And I want to be clear: I get it. Writing is hard. It can be solitary. It can be frustrating. There’s a lot of people out there who are not professional writers and who don’t want to have to write certain things, like a “just looping back” email to a boss or to a coworker. Last week I had to write an email to a pest-control company about being overcharged for a mousetrap. It’s not sexy stuff. There’s a lot of writing out there that is drudgery. It is the exact stuff that artificial intelligence automates really, really well. So listen; I get it. And there’s also the issue here that, as a writer, I may not be the most impartial messenger. Of course, I’m gonna be defensive about this. I don’t want something to come out there and take the one marketable skill that I have and just make it irrelevant. But I also think that there’s something else going on here. Something that goes well beyond what precious writerly types like myself think. Because writing isn’t just a boring, overly formal thing that we have to do to get through school. It’s an essential form of communication. There’s a lot of debate over whether writing is synonymous with thinking, and well, I’m not sure that it’s one-to-one here. Writing, and especially learning to write effectively, can be a powerful skill that helps advance or sharpen our thinking. Writing is a way to organize our thoughts. It’s a way to set aside quiet, focused time to understand what we really believe about something. It is the scaffolding on which we build arguments, which means that it is, in part, a way to crystallize how we see the world. What I noticed most in the response to this Wall Street Journal op-ed and all the backlash was this group of people arguing that this is where things are going. The toothpaste can’t be put back in the tube, and so we might as well embrace it. It’s a “resistance is futile” type of argument. But the thing about new technologies is that they’re never inevitable. Nor is it written in stone that professional writers and news organizations and people who rely on the trust of their audience to sell a product that is quite literally the craft of written argumentation need to roll over completely without negotiating their own boundaries in this moment. To automate our drudgery is understandable. But to automate such a broad form of communication—to outsource an important tool for working past the roadblocks that exist in our own minds—that’s something different than embracing a new gadget. And so when this op-ed came out, I saw all the backlash—all the “This is akin to using a calculator,” “This is like spellcheck,” “This is like Grammarly,” “Please stop all the hand-wringing.” I wanted to go deeper. I could imagine this grim future where these tools take over almost completely, and where writing becomes this strange, somewhat vestigial skill. In that future, I think humanity is worse off. Now, I can also imagine a future where AI-assisted writing is more akin to a tool. Maybe it’s something that someday breaks open a new hybrid form of the craft. Is there a world where chatbots can help people to learn how to structure arguments better, to enhance their vocabularies, to learn how to spot good writing from bad, and make us better communicators in the stuff that matters, while also automating all the little stuff? I’m skeptical, but it doesn’t mean that it’s not possible. So, what does the future of AI writing look like? I wanted to bring on some other writers who are living through this moment and who crucially have different experiences doing different kinds of professional writing. Teddy Brown is a reporter at CNN, and in a previous life he worked in the tech industry as a technical writer, doing a lot of that for-hire, less sexy writing that’s under threat from Claude and ChatGPT. He wrote an essay titled “The End of the Working Writer” that gets at a lot of this stuff. Elizabeth Spiers is a contributing opinion writer at The New York Times, the host of Slate’s Money podcast, and a digital strategist who also teaches writing. You may also know her as the founding editor of Gawker. She recently wrote a column with the evocative title “Why You Shouldn’t Use AI to Write a Professional Op-Ed, by Me, a Professional Op-Ed Writer Who Has Less Real Competition If You Do.” So how should we think about all this? Teddy and Elizabeth join me now to talk about all of it. Teddy, Elizabeth—thank you for coming on Galaxy Brain. Welcome. Warzel: So I want to start here with a baseline. You’re both professional writers, so walk me through a little bit your relationship to generative AI and chatbots as it relates to your life and work here. Writing, drafting, research, cheap Google assistant. Teddy, I’ll start with you. Just how are you using this in your life, or not using it? Brown: Yeah, so I actually don’t use it at all for any of my professional stuff. It’s funny; I came from tech before. I’ve become staff at CNN now, but that was sort of only five months ago. But before that, all my day jobs were in tech, when I used AI quite a lot. Just because it was sort of coming down from a lot of leadership that this is the tool we’re gonna use. And then I’ve shifted over during my tenure at CNN to not using them at all. So it’s become sort of—you see the differences emerge pretty quickly about how your process works. And I was a freelancer for a long time as well, so I always kept sort of a partition up between my journalistic work and, you know, my day-job work, when I would use AI tools in my day job but not use them for my journalistic work. Spiers: Yeah, so I’m a writer, and I co-host Slate Money, which is Slate’s finance-news podcast of the week. So I have to—you know, I write about AI. We sometimes podcast about AI. So I use the tools partly to understand them. But also, I work in a lot of newsrooms, as a consultant, and so I’ve been responsible in some cases for introducing AI to help automate workflows, or to do certain types of data journalism. So I’m not anti-AI. But I do not use it to write. It’s not very helpful for research, I found, because it’s sort of worse than baseline Google in terms of what it produces. So I’m happy to leave it entirely out of my writing process. Warzel: Inside newsrooms, what are some of the ways that you’ve seen this stuff come into a newsroom and really help the journalistic process? Spiers: Where I’ve used it more directly, it’s been for automating workflows. If you work in any kind of large newsroom, you know that we’re dealing with a bunch of different pieces of software that very often are cobbled together by processes that are heavily, you know, human oriented, and they can be tedious. And a lot of it’s just using things that are badly connected to other things. And you can use AI to automate some of that stuff, which takes the pressure off of, I think, journalists to keep doing things that are tedious and administrative, as opposed to actually going out and reporting or creating stories and columns and, you know—the things that are actually hard to do. Warzel: Right. And there is that that difference between the research tool—the cheap, you know, Google replacement—and the, like, “I have this corpus of information; it’s so massive.” Or it’s, you know, scattered in all these different places. The ability to go and and scan through that type of stuff, or collate or organize, like those daisy-chaining, really tedious tasks together to make something else, is I think a sort of an underlooked version of that. Which, I think a lot of journalists tend to be industry-wide very split on AI stuff. And that’s sort of what I want to get into here, especially as we just talk about writing. So we’re basically—November will be four years into the ChatGPT era. AI writing’s obviously flooded the internet. We have the book-publishing industry, which, according to a lot of reporting, is in utter chaos. Book deals falling through. I had someone in the book industry tell me recently, “Don’t write one now.” Like, don’t pitch one, don’t—just, like, stop on books for like six months, please. Because the industry has no idea what the hell to do, and they’re just terrified that they’re gonna be getting AI-generated work. And then you have, further in the world of professional writing, something like the billionaire Stanley Druckenmiller, who recently published an op-ed in The Wall Street Journal identified as 100 percent written by AI. And both him and the head of the Journal’s opinion page admitted to and defended the usage of chatbots. The head of the opinion page [Paul Gigot] wrote in his own op-ed—who knows if he wrote that himself—that “AI is a fact of modern life.” Right after that, Semafor did some reporting, showed that an analysis of 310 guest-writer op-eds in the last six months in The Wall Street Journal, Washington Post, and The New York Times—one in six used AI, according to Pangram, the AI detector. Now, Pangram is in its own way, you know—we’re not a hundred percent sure about the accuracy there. But it seems like there’s a lot here. And I normally start all these conversations that work toward the big thing, but let’s do the big thing first. Like, help me understand what’s going on here. Like, are we cooked? Spiers: I think it depends on what kind of writing we’re talking about. I wrote a little ranty thing on Medium about the Gigot/Druckenmiller situation, and it was really directed specifically to people who wanna publish professional op-eds. And that’s actually not where, you know, I think the big problem is. Because, for the most part, the Druckenmiller kind of situation, part of that’s coming too from the way that these op-ed pages work. Where the problem is not about AI. But I do see people using AI for what I think of as “commodity content” a lot. You know, you go on LinkedIn, and you see the same kind of style of post that’s, you know, “Here’s what my divorce taught me about B2B sales,” or something of that variety. And, you know, that’s people sort of learning to write with a formula. But that’s also—you can sort of tell when they’re using AI. And I think AI can replicate formulaic, unoriginal stuff really well. So if that’s what you’re doing, then you probably are cooked. But I also think that there is still a premium for original ideas, and I don’t think that AI is really capable of coming up with that. And the writing is that it produces is very cliché-ridden and formulaic, because it’s being trained on a corpus of data that for the most part is, you know, mostly mediocre. And there’s just no way to kind of prompt an AI into the sort of creativity or sort of holistic understanding of what it’s outputting the way that you can with a human and human editors. Brown: I always tell people that it’s not the tics that these things have. It’s not use of cliché, it’s not lists of three. It’s not, you know, it’s not X or Y. It’s that these words don’t mean anything. Like, you read the entire piece, and like—I didn’t learn anything from here. This is all hollow. That’s what AI writing actually is. Warzel: Right. So a feature of the dustup with the Wall Street Journal thing, which I actually think is much more important than the op-ed itself, is that lack of shame that we’re talking about here. Right? I think it’s the reason that so many people paid attention to it. If there was, you know, a tail-between-the-legs kind of thing, it would be like, Yeah, you know; somebody else got caught. We don’t do this. Do you think we’re creeping toward, like, a post-shame AI-writing era here? Does this mark for you all an actual departure, or is this just a couple of guys who were, you know, probably going in this direction anyway? Spiers: I think there’s already a kind of post-shame stance from a lot of the writing community that’s not really institutionalized. And I don’t think that matters as much. But I think if you’re talking about a news organization—where trust matters, the relationship with the reader matters—I think at the very least you owe the reader a disclosure that AI was used in the piece. And you know, I’m a contributing writer for The New York Times, and my contract explicitly states that I cannot use AI. Even for, you know, putting it in to copy edit anything; not allowed to do that. In fact, if even if I’m gonna write about AI, I’m supposed to get permission to use the tools first. So the Times is sort of at the exact opposite end of the, I think, restrictive side of what people are and aren’t supposed to do. But until Paul Gigot wrote this editorial, everyone I know at The Wall Street Journal was under the impression that you could not do your work with AI that way, either. So I think it may have some backfiring effect on them—that first of all. they’re going to get deluged with AI-written submissions. But also they’re going to have to clarify where that line is. And so far, they haven’t. Brown: Yeah. I think that there’s a few arguments I’ve seen emerging. And one I’m not entirely unsympathetic to is that if the writing is good, who cares? And like, I understand where that comes from. Like, I think it’s actually beholden on readers and critics and things to be like, That writing sucks. and I don’t care if a human wrote it or if an AI wrote it. And now it just so happens that the balance of the vast majority of AI writing is awful, and that you gotta be able to identify it. And I know that it puts a lot of pressure on readers, who are just inundated with all of this slop, basically. But it is a little bit like, we should be a little more critical of what we’re putting in front of our faces and absorbing. Warzel: I understand what you’re saying about having some—you know, you gotta put a little bit of an onus, too, on the reader to care about it. To vote with your eyeballs, etcetera. But to me, it feels like this is like a frustration that I have with the conversation around this op-ed. and around the feeling, too, from certain people who are just like: This is inevitable. This is where it’s gonna go. The internet is gonna flood with this stuff. So, like, we’re gonna look back on the hand-wringing about this and kind of laugh one day, right? It’s gonna feel super-quaint, all you people who are doing this. And that may be true. Like, that I may be on the losing end of this thing, right? Like everyone’s just gonna be doing slop stuff, and it’s gonna get more sophisticated, and we’ll figure out some way forward. But for me, there’s also—in any era of technological change, you actually need people who are going to protest it. Who are going to stand and thwart it and stop it and object to it. Because that’s also how we create the new norms for this stuff, right? If we don’t spend some time thinking about that and having some people actually push back, if nobody pushes back, then it just happens. And it happens however these companies want. Or whatever the, you know, the lowest common denominator of slop that can get published. I find I’m really frustrated by the idea that some of these institutions, like the whole point of being the institution is to stand up for, you know, institutional things. Spiers: Yeah, what’s crazy about this is that, first of all, it’s The Wall Street Journal doing it. Where you have one of the most, what business-side people would call “premium products in the space.” And you’re actively enshittifying your own product when what you’re selling is, you know, “the original thoughts of Stanley Druckenmiller”—not what he couldn’t be bothered to slap into a, you know, Google Doc himself, but produced via AI. Your value proposition, even on the business side, is eroded by that. So I think there’s a lot of short-term thinking about headcount and cost. That really, people are using AI in ways that are not necessarily additive at the expense of the long-term value proposition of their own products. Brown: I do wonder about this sometimes, because we’re in the media. And we exist in sort of a filter bubble of our own making, right? I’m like, do people in general audiences care that Druckenmiller uses AI? Does the 85 percent of people who do not care about writing and actually find writing a chore—that they don’t want to write a note about why they’re gonna be five minutes late; they’d rather press a button and have that happen for them—actually care that much? And I worry about that, because I care so much about writing. Spiers: I think you’re right, but I also think with newsrooms, we have a trust problem already. And I think the readers care if they think they’re being deceived. I don’t think they really care about, you know, the process of writing, or writing for its own sake. Warzel: I also think alongside the trust part of it, too, there’s an effort thing that I think is actually very real, that comes with the part of the trust. Like if people, you know, can’t be arsed to, like, do the work, and everyone else is like, That’s fine, yeah; just send that sucker through, right? There’s this idea of like—so where else do they cut corners? Teddy, you wrote a good piece earlier this summer that was titled “The End of the Working Writer.” And I want to get to that, but the piece was kind of kicked off or inspired, perhaps, by a post by Bloomberg’s Joe Weisenthal, who’s constantly posting on X. This post set off, you know, a debate online. And he wrote: “Unfortunately, I think in the near future, not using LLMs to write for you will be like someone refusing to use Google Maps for directions in a new city. A bizarre, idiosyncratic choice that’s just completely incomprehensible to the vast majority of people.” So first I just wanted to ask: Do you think that that prediction is right, here? Do you think that’s kind of where we’re heading? Brown: I think “the vast majority of people” might be a little bit of an understatement. I think that as adoption picks up for a lot of these tools, I do think those people who, again, don’t really care about writing that much in the process of writing, and view it as a chore they can replace with a few clicks or a few keystrokes. Yeah—I think that’s gonna be quite a lot of people are going to start acting that way. And it’s just ease of doing these sort of things. We’re interested in the process of writing for its own sake, and in a way to sort of unpack our own thoughts and think about the problems we’re trying to solve. But yeah—I unfortunately see this becoming a little bit more acceptable to just use these chats for writing anything you need. Warzel: Teddy, you mentioned earlier that you—and that’s part of this Substack post that you did—is that you used to do this kind of work. And a good bit of your argument in “The End of the Working Writer” is that the AI-and-writing conversation can get caught up in this, like, craft/artistic corridor—but that a lot of writing’s very utilitarian. It’s very technical, right? It’s this stuff: Press the button and say you’re gonna be five minutes late. Or it is this, you know, copywriting; you’re writing white papers, or product descriptions. Copy that’s gonna be used for search-engine optimization. Corporate speak. LinkedIn posts, Elizabeth, as you said. And since you did this kind of work, you argue maybe this conversation is not talking enough about how AI is changing that brand of writing. So tell me about the working writer a little bit—define it better for me, and what AI is doing to that category of writing. Brown: Yeah. I mean, it’s all writing that no one cares about, is really what it comes down to. But I did this work because it was well compensated, and I had to afford rent in New York. And this is my career for a long time. I think that what happens is that AI is very good at writing that doesn’t matter. All the things you were talking about—all those pieces of little pieces of internet copy that are not meant to actually be critically engaged with—AI is extremely good at writing those things. And so there are two forces happening. One is that people recognize that this writing is actually not worth that much anymore. And so they’re like, How can we do this for the smallest cost possible? And you have these enormous costs of AI integrations into technology companies generally, where a lot of the better-paid opportunities were. Daniel Kolitz had a great piece in The Baffler today that’s talking exactly about this. Like it is there is this sort of squeezing-out of what I compared to session musicians in music. It’s like—Well, if I don’t need to pay these four people to play the backing track for this commercial thing; I can just plug into Suno and get the same thing, basically. It’s like, Why do I need to pay them $10,000? And like, right. That was paying those four people’s rent for the next couple months. And so I really worry about this hollowing out a generation of writers who are, you know, sort of 10 years behind me, who don’t have these opportunities to work on writing that is not necessarily the most wonderful artistic, literary pursuit, but is a way to at least make a respectable living in a very expensive city. And now those jobs are just gonna be gone. Warzel: Let me just play devil’s advocate here. There are probably people who don’t live in New York City who are, you know, gonna listen in and say: Well, if it’s work that fundamentally doesn’t have any value, and it’s a good thing—like, what do we lose if we lose this? Brown: I think it’s a big question. Because I was thinking about this before I got on, and sort of the idea of like—why, if you’re gonna take the job away from like 50 farmers with using mechanical tools or something. It’s like, Well, should we not do that? Should we not advance past this in order to sort of create more? Why would we keep paying people to do this writing no one actually cares about? And I understand that argument, sort of, in the medium and long term. But in the short term, it’s doing actual material damage to people who are trying to feed themselves. Spiers: I just worry that AI is making everybody dumber. Spiers: I think when you outsource the fundamental cognitive arsenal that you’re using to actually construct a good argument, or to make a piece of art, what happens to us as a society if everybody’s outsourcing that to AI models? I see this a little bit with, you know, I have an 11-year-old son in school. They’ve sort of—most of he and his peers all know how to use ChatGPT to ask a question. And it’s increasingly difficult for people in academic settings to get their students to do the rigorous work of sitting down and trying to think through an argument without these aids. And I think it may badly prepare students for the real world, where they may not have that assistance all the time. Or they may have to learn how to think through more complex things that are just gonna be far more difficult, because they haven’t developed that intellectual rigor that comes from having to work your way through things from scratch. Brown: I feel that as an adult. When I worked in tech, I was writing 3,000-page papers about really dense B2B-enterprise marketing strategies, or whatever. Brown: Yeah, exactly. And I would get to the end, and it would go onto the website: I have no idea what I wrote about. I could not tell you one thing about that, because it was just all flowing through Claude. And all I was doing was editing line by line, and making sure it sounded good. But I could not tell you anything about what I actually wrote, which is weird. It’s a weird sensation. Warzel: This gets to—I wanted to get here, which is that the broader “we” doesn’t think of writing, I think, as a technology. But it is. It’s an amazing technology that allows us to put our ideas in other people’s heads, in this intimate, transformative way. And then there’s this question of what it does for us, right? So I wanted to read something from the publication Nature about what might be going on when we write, that I enjoyed, here. Which is that: “Writing compels us to think not in the chaotic, nonlinear way that our minds typically wander, but in a structured, intentional manner. By writing it down, we can sort years of research, data, and analysis into an actual story, thereby identifying our main message and the influence of our work. This is not merely a philosophical observation; it is backed by scientific evidence.” And so I wanted to ask you all, as writers: Is the act of writing thinking? Or is it something different? Is it just something that forces us to think so that we may write? And I wanna get your thoughts on this, because I think it’s necessary and useful to put a point on what we are losing cognitively when we outsource it. Spiers: I think it is thinking. It’s not the only form of thinking, but there’s plenty of research that kind of indicates that there are parts of your brain that are activated when you’re constructing something as a written sentence, versus other types of, you know, communicative expressions. There’s also a lot of anthropological history that indicates that when humans started developing language, it changed the way that they were cognitively structured. So I think if you remove that, I just think the additive benefits of it probably just go away, and their systemic effects on your cognition altogether. You know, maybe it gets replaced by something else. I don’t know. But, you know, I wouldn’t rule out the possibility that whatever we’re losing can’t be replaced in the future. But I think for right now, we know that it serves a purpose. And we don’t have anything to replace it. So that does concern me. Brown: I mean, of course writing is thinking. It’s thinking through issues, and trying to sort of figure out what you’re actually trying to say, or trying to write down. I mean, it is like it’s actively trying to entangle problems that you’re dealing with. Instead of being like, you can just use incredibly seductive tools like Claude and ChatGPT, ’cause you can just put in a problem and it gives you a solution. And I know that people will say you can have it challenge you, and you can turn things and press these knobs, and all this kind of stuff. But it is also outsourcing that path for you. Warzel: I’ve thought a lot about this, because I’ve had some people challenge me on the “writing is thinking” thing. Like, are you actually doing that? And I would say if I had to, you know, really be precise about it—maybe it’s not just the act of thinking. But there’s no other time when I can get myself alone in a space where I am forced to think in order to write on a micro, you know, sentence-by-sentence, piece-of-logic-by-piece-of-logic basis, right? Like, I am having to have an extremely honest conversation with myself. Of like: Did that make sense? Does that connect to this in a way? And I think when people say, like, “Yes, man, it’s such a good sparring partner.” Claude, ChatGPT—it’s so good at, like you said, coming up with a counterargument or, you know, taking down your straw-man arguments, whatever it is. I think, you know, maybe, that is true in some way. But I don’t think it’s as good as having to deal with the honesty of yourself—where you’re like, I have this idea in the back of my head. I need to communicate that on the page. Does what’s on the page match that abstract idea in my brain? And like frankly, I think the act of writing is reconciling that with yourself. And that ability to be alone, like it forces you actually to do a lot of—you know, people find writing so agonizing, sometimes, on the page. Because it’s like, you have to be honest with yourself. Like, Is that my actual idea? Is that, you know, is that any good? Is that worth anything? And that conversation, to me—that’s what I think we’re losing. Brown: Well, and the models themselves, I mean the learning libraries force any problem that you’re trying to interrogate through one of the chats to look only backward. They can only look at things that have already happened. And I think it limits the amount of novelty you can have in an argument. It limits the paths you can take with it. And I know that Silicon Valley’s very antagonistic toward interiority and introspection right now, but the only way you can get there is thinking about: How do I feel about this? What does this do to me? What am I curious about, and how does this make me feel? And that doesn’t exist in a learning library; that doesn’t exist in a chatbot. Spiers: Also just at a macro level, now that you mentioned that, I think Silicon Valley has a general anti-intellectualism orientation that sort of gets built into the tech. It’s: I don’t wanna have to, you know, use my precious brain cells on going through rigorous material that might be difficult. I’m gonna build a piece of software that’s gonna do it for me. And I think the long-term ramifications of that are just sort of bad for society. There’s a cult in Silicon Valley of trying to learn things very quickly, but without friction, you know? And I think if you take those things away, what you’re doing is something that kind of looks surface-level like intellectual development—but isn’t when you scratch the surface. It lacks the rigor, the complexity, all of that. Warzel: Ezra Klein had a phrase that I liked with this. Which is “the Matrix theory of the mind.” Right? With the idea that, like, some people see information as the Matrix thing, right? You just—you plug, you jack into the, you know, the Borg. And you upload the information, and like: Boom; I understand everything about Kant. Right? Like, I know how to, you know, fly this F-15. Whatever it is. And it’s like the actual process of learning tends to be extremely different. Which is, it’s just about how you, your brain grapple with material, with information, and you think. And I think it’s the disagreement that Silicon Valley has between one style of learning and the other. I wanted to bring up, though, the argument with all of this with AI-assisted writing. There are folks that have argued that there’s an accessibility issue here. That not all writers either could, you know, can afford to have human editing, or that some brains are requiring outside help or accommodations. Chatbots assist with this. And another way of saying this—without getting into a conversation just about ability or disability—is that not everyone can write well. And not everyone wants to write well. And certainly, most people aren’t professional writers. So how should we be thinking about these tools for a crowd of people that aren’t pros? Brown: I think there’s a lot of people who see writing as an intellectual costume they can sort of put on, and chat like these chatbots allow them to do that in a very seamless, frictionless way. I’ve been on the record saying this. I think there should be fewer writers. I think there are way too many people who call themselves writers, and should not be in the industry generally. I think there’s sort of—it’s flooded with that. It might be good in the long run, because people who are actually good at this might be able to separate themselves. Spiers: I think, also, it’s sort of—I think people have this simplistic idea of writing. That, you know, anyone can do it. Just because anybody can sit down and type and do a document, or most people can. But I can’t think of—there’s a whole range of other professional skills that if I said, I’m really bad at this, but I wanna be able to do it, people would look at me like I had three heads. Like, if I wanted to go play WNBA basketball right now, I’m 5’1” and 49. That’s not gonna happen, and there’s no number of tools that I could add to myself that would get me there. And so I do think, also, people do need to understand that writing is a specialized skill. You know, it doesn’t matter. I think if you want to do it professionally, that you’re good at it. If you don’t care about doing it professionally, no one’s gonna stop you from writing for your own enjoyment or writing on a blog. I mean that the internet has completely democratized that. You can publish at almost zero cost. Warzel: You expressed it perfectly—that there are so many other genres of things where if you’re like, I want to do this professionally, and I’m not good at it, the people are tough, right? But what’s interesting and frustrating about the conversation with writing is that we always seem to, culturally, regard it differently. Like clearly—writing is a craft. It’s an art form, but it’s also this thing that everyone does, right? Often in these quotidian ways as we’re talking about. And so I think it’s treated differently than other crafts. And what I was thinking about in this space was Hollywood and, you know, AI video-generation tools. Like the guilds in Hollywood go out on strike, in part because they want protections from the studios from AI, and standards. And for the most part, people don’t say, like, “In a couple of years, of the all the hand-wringing”—you know, like all these people are being precious. “They’re gonna look back on this and cringe at what they were doing.” And I think that’s because most people aren’t out there every day making movies and sending them to their boss, right? Like, the way that they write an email or a LinkedIn post. And so I’m wondering: Do you guys think that writing is more at risk than other creative crafts to AI because of its universal nature? Brown: I certainly think the large middle class of it is, for sure. In the same way that I think the large middle class of moviemaking or the large middle class of graphic designers are at risk. I do think there is a huge contingent of this sort of middle 80 percent of writers who have to make ends meet, not through prestige, legacy media outlets. And those are at huge amount of risk. Spiers: I think some of it, too, is just that the skill set that you use in writing—and all of the tools available to you, and all of the things that you learn—live entirely in your head. You know, if you’re a filmmaker and you have a wide set of skills, this also involves using things that people see externally as being highly technical. If you’re a writer, you’re using a laptop, which everybody has. And so the idea that there’s a barrier to entry to writing—people don’t kind of understand that. Because they’re like, Well, I could write something and put it on the internet, and what’s the difference? So I think anything that has a kind of zero barrier to entry, people tend to overestimate the ease of doing it. Warzel: I think we’re all trying to be mindful of, you know, we are in our bubble of “this matters to us.” So as writers, are there opportunities here? You know, like there’s a few excerpts of media that I’ve read where people just start like, you know, talking to the chatbot in the middle of the thing as part of it; it’s almost like a creative-fiction-y thing. I’m just curious if you all see the possibility for opportunities here that aren’t just, like, I’m kinda gonna give a very bland prompt and then get out, you know, a bunch of Claude lorem ipsum. Brown: There’s certainly—it’s an edge case for me. The sort of avant-garde being like, I think there’s people wanting to read your prompts about how you got something. I think that’s actually like a fascinating artistic case. Like, I don’t care about your writing. Show me what your prompt looks like—because that’s more interesting to me, because it actually shows what your thinking is. I think that what the most likely scenario for me. Or—I don’t know if this is most likely or sort of what I would prefer to happen, is that professional writing becomes more akin to vinyl collecting now. And that this is sort of—not a curiosity, but something for people who really care about it. And that they actually seek out, you know, good writing done by humans. Just because the flood sadly is already here. I don’t want to be too fatalistic, but it’s just gonna be the state of things where it’s just like: This is too easy. You know it’s too big. The tools are too entrenched. And this is sort of—we have to figure out ways to live with that. Warzel: Elizabeth, where do you fall on that? Spiers: I mean I think that you know, if we do end up in a scenario where we’re flooded with AI writing and it’s all crap, that actually makes people who can do things that AI can’t more valuable. Like, for example, AI can’t write jokes. It’s terrible at comedy. And partly because it doesn’t have a kind of meta-sensibility, it’s incapable of understanding the sort of intersection between absurdity and novelty, which is where humor comes from. So I’m not terribly worried in the short term. I think there is a possibility that we eventually end up with models that are good enough that they can produce novelty, and that they do produce interesting output. And then, I don’t know what happens. You know, I think we’ll all probably adapt to it in different ways. Right now I’m not thinking about that far ahead, about any of it. Warzel: I feel sort of both sides of this. Where, to me—I said this on a different podcast, but I’m a really bad emailer. In the sense that, like, no punctuation or caps or things like that. And I’ve always had— Warzel: Haha, yeah; I guess, yeah. Just a wonderful comparison to make there. But that’s, like, my email style, and it’s embarrassing, and it’s always been. And I’ve had people comment recently, like: “You know what, man. I know you sent that email. Because it’s hot garbage.” Like, Thank you. Thank you so much for not taking the time to— Warzel: Yeah, exactly. It’s authentic. And I see that, you know, more in responses to just my work, personally, and things like that. Like, people feeling that that level of craft as like, as a distinction, right. And so I feel like there is that way which, in the near term, it can really distinguish. But I just wanted to ask both of you to close out here. If you were made writing czar today and you could set some kind of lists of standards that you would like to see codified for professional writing. In terms of this technology disclosure, all that stuff. Like—what would be just a couple of your first steps? Just things that like, boilerplate, “This is the norm that we wanna impose around AI and writing.” Spiers: At minimum, I think disclosure has to be a norm. Like, you know, even if newsrooms get to the point where they decide every single contributor can use AI—including to construct an argument—not informing the reader I think is a mistake. Because then readers will easily assume that all of the work of the newsroom is outsourced to AI, and therefore it’s sort of commoditized and not that original. And I think it’s a trust issue. Brown: I think Elizabeth brings up a really good point, and the journalism thing is a perfect example of this. That there are those cracks in foundational trust for institutions. Listen, there’s a lot of trust issues generally, but putting yet another one into the dam is—it’s really difficult to sort of navigate ourselves away from. On an individual level, for writers, I mean, I hate to say it, but it just doesn’t have a place in my sort of toolkit as a journalist. By sort of filter, you know—Google searches. Sure, Gemini is in my life every day because it’s the top of the search bar. But just, after using it so much in my previous life, I have just sort of been like: This is actually quite freeing to do all this work myself again. And maybe that’s personal; maybe that’s sort of me going from one sort of hard side to the other. But there is something so rewarding about finding stuff out yourself. And I really love that part of reporting. Warzel: I think that’s a great place to leave it. Teddy, Elizabeth—thank you for coming on and talking about this weird place that we find ourselves in, with this strange typing thing that we started doing a long time ago, that now robots do. But thank you for coming on. Warzel: If you liked what you saw here, new episodes of Galaxy Brain drop every Friday. And if you want to support this work, and the work of my fellow colleagues, you can subscribe to the publication at TheAtlantic.com/Listener. That’s TheAtlantic.com/Listener.
